Kept identical to the reference implementation apart from returning\n104\t    the baseline as well (used for the diagnostic plots).\n105\t    \"\"\"\n106\t    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\n107\t    win = int(win_baseline * fs)\n108\t    if baseline == 'maximin':\n109\t        Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline])\n110\t        Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n111\t        Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n112\t    elif baseline == 'constant':\n113\t        Flow = np.amin(gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline]))\n114\t    else:\n115\t        Flow = 0.\n116\t    return Fc - Flow, Flow\n117\t\n118\t\n119\tdef bin_average(x, k=None):\n120\t    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of k consecutive samples along the last axis.\n121\t\n122\t    Trailing samples that do not fill a bin are dropped.\n123\t    \"\"\"\n124\t    k = BIN_FRAMES if k is None else k\n125\t    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n126\t    return x[..., :n].reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n127\t\n128\t\n129\t# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n130\t# behaviour processing\n131\t# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n132\tdef load_motion_energy(session_dir, n_frames):\n133\t    \"\"\"Motion energy on the imaging frame grid.\n134\t\n135\t    The camera is triggered by the 2-photon acquisition, so camera frame i\n136\t    corresponds to imaging frame i as long as no trigger was dropped.  Dropped\n137\t    triggers show up as inter-frame intervals that are an integer multiple of the\n138\t    nominal one; the cumulative sum of the rounded intervals therefore gives the\n139\t    imaging-frame index of every camera frame.  Missing values (dropped frames, and\n140\t    the first frame, for which no frame difference exists) are linearly interpolated,\n141\t    as suggested by the dataset README.\n142\t\n143\t    Returns (motion_energy (n_frames,), invalid mask (n_frames,) bool).\n144\t    \"\"\"\n145\t    md = os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve')\n146\t    me = np.load(os.path.join(md, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n147\t    itv = np.load(os.path.join(md, 'interframe_int.npy'))\n148\t    assert len(itv) == len(me) - 1\n149\t\n150\t    nominal = np.median(itv)\n151\t    steps = np.round(itv / nominal).astype(np.int64)\n152\t    idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(steps)])\n153\t\n154\t    out = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\n155\t    keep = idx < n_frames                    # a few sessions record past the last 2p frame\n156\t    out[idx[keep]] = me[keep]\n157\t    out[0] = np.nan                          # me[0] is a placeholder (no previous frame)\n158\t\n159\t    missing = np.isnan(out)\n160\t    if missing.all():\n161\t        raise ValueError(f'{session_dir}: no motion energy could be aligned')\n162\t    out[missing] = np.interp(np.flatnonzero(missing), np.flatnonzero(~missing),\n163\t                             out[~missing])\n164\t    return out, missing\n165\t\n166\t\n167\tdef discretize_quantiles(x, n_bins=N_QUANTILES):\n168\t    \"\"\"Assign each value of x to one of n_bins equal-percentile bins.\n169\t\n170\t    Edges are the n_bins-1 interior quantiles of x itself, so the bins hold equal\n171\t    numbers of samples (up to ties).  Returns (labels int64, edges).\n172\t    \"\"\"\n173\t    qs = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)[1:-1]\n174\t    edges = np.percentile(x, qs)\n175\t    labels = np.searchsorted(edges, x, side='right').astype(np.int64)\n176\t    return labels, edges\n177\t\n178\t\n179\t# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n180\t# per-session conversion\n181\t# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n182\tdef convert_session(session_dir, neural_scaling='center', verbose=True, neucoeff=None):\n183\t    \"\"\"Convert one session to trial lists.\n184\t\n185\t    neural_scaling: how the dF/F is conditioned for the decoder.  For a linear\n186\t        readout with a bias term (which is what the provided decoder fits on top of\n187\t        the per-session projection) subtracting a per-neuron constant and dividing\n188\t        the whole session by one scalar are information-preserving; they only change\n189\t        the conditioning of the optimisation and of the SVD initialisation.\n190\t        'none'   : dF/F as returned by f_processing (arbitrary fluorescence units)\n191\t        'global' : dF/F divided by one scalar per session (its pooled s.d.)\n192\t        'center' : (default) additionally subtract each neuron's session mean, so\n193\t                   that the projection is initialised with the SVD of the\n194\t                   *fluctuations* rather than of the (large, positive) mean offset\n195\t        'zscore' : each neuron's dF/F z-scored within the session; this also\n196\t                   re-weights neurons by 1/s.d., which is not information preserving\n197\t    \"\"\"\n198\t    t0 = time.time()\n199\t    subject = os.path.basename(os.path.dirname(session_dir))\n200\t    sess_name = os.path.basename(session_dir)\n201\t\n202\t    F, Fneu, fs, n_frames, t1\n212\t\n213\t    # --- behaviour: motion energy on the imaging grid, then 10-frame averaging ---\n214\t    me, me_missing = load_motion_energy(session_dir, n_frames)\n215\t    me_binned = bin_average(me)\n216\t    # fraction of interpolated (dropped) camera frames contributing to each bin\n217\t    missing_binned = bin_average(me_missing.astype(np.float64))\n218\t\n219\t    # --- cut into trials ---\n220\t    bins_per_trial = int(round(TRIAL_SEC * fs / BIN_FRAMES))\n221\t    n_trials = dff_binned.shape[1] // bins_per_trial\n222\t    n_used = n_trials * bins_per_trial\n223\t    dff_binned = dff_binned[:, :n_used]\n224\t    me_binned = me_binned[:n_used]\n225\t    missing_binned = missing_binned[:n_used]\n226\t\n227\t    # --- neural scaling (see CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5) ---\n228\t    if neural_scaling == 'global':\n229\t        scale = float(dff_binned.std())\n230\t        neural_all = dff_binned / scale\n231\t    elif neural_scaling == 'center':\n232\t        scale = float(dff_binned.std())\n233\t        neural_all = (dff_binned - dff_binned.mean(axis=1, keepdims=True)) / scale\n234\t    elif neural_scaling == 'zscore':\n235\t        mu = dff_binned.mean(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n236\t        sd = dff_binned.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n237\t        neural_all = (dff_binned - mu) / np.maximum(sd, 1e-6)\n238\t        scale = None\n239\t    elif neural_scaling == 'none':\n240\t        neural_all = dff_binned\n241\t        scale = 1.0\n242\t    else:\n243\t        raise ValueError(neural_scaling)\n244\t    neural_all = neural_all.astype(np.float32)\n245\t\n246\t    # --- output: quintiles of motion energy, thresholds per session ---\n247\t    labels_all, edges = discretize_quantiles(me_binned)\n248\t\n249\t    # --- [], [], []\n254\t    for tr in range(n_trials):\n255\t        sl = slice(tr * bins_per_trial, (tr + 1) * bins_per_trial)\n256\t        neural.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural_all[:, sl]))\n257\t        inputs.append(time_all[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n258\t        outputs.append(labels_all[sl][None, :])\n259\t\n260\t    label, p0 = SUBJECT_INFO[subject]\n261\t    day_idx = list_sessions(subject).index(session_dir)\n262\t    info = {\n263\t        'subject': subject,\n264\t        'mouse_label': label,\n265\t        'session': sess_name,\n266\t        'date': sess_name[:10],\n267\t        'day_index': day_idx,\n268\t        'postnatal_day': p0 + day_idx,\n269\t        'n_neurons': int(neural_all.shape[0]),\n270\t        'n_frames': int(n_frames),\n271\t        'fs': fs,\n272\t        'neucoeff': neucoeff,\n273\t        'n_trials': int(n_trials),\n274\t        'session_duration_s': n_frames / fs,\n275\t        'frac_frames_interpolated': 'dff_scale_divisor': scale,\n279\t        'frac_bins_with_interpolated_frames': float((missing_binned > 0).mean()),\n280\t    }\n281\t    if verbose:\n282\t        print(f'  {subject}/{sess_name}: the Majnik et al. 2025 (eLife 107540, Track2p) longitudinal 2-photon dataset\ninto the decoder-compatible pickle format.\n\nDataset: 6 mice (jm031, jm032, jm038, jm039, jm040, jm046), 41 daily sessions of\nspontaneous activity in L2/3 barrel cortex (P7-P14), GCaMP8m imaged at 30 Hz, with\nsimultaneous 30 Hz videography quantified as 'motion energy'.  The suite2p folders\nreleased with the paper already contain only the Track2p-tracked, iscell-curated\nneurons, row-matched across the days of a mouse.\n\nDecoder task\n    input  : time elapsed from the beginning of the session (s), time varying\n    output : motion energy, discretised into 5 equal-percentile (quintile) bins,\n             thresholds chosen per session, time varying\n    trials : the continuous session is cut into consecutive 60 s trials\n\nProcessing (follows the paper / reference code, see CONVERSION_NOTES.md):\n    * dF/F  = maximin baseline-corrected fluorescence, computed exactly as\n      track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing with suite2p default parameters\n      (neucoeff from ops = 0.7, sig_baseline = 10 frames, win_baseline = 60 s).\n    * both dF/F and motion energy are averaged in bins of 10 consecutive frames\n      (333.33 ms), as the paper does \"for all decoding analysis\".\n    * motion energy is put back on the imaging frame grid using the camera\n      inter-frame intervals (the camera is triggered by the microscope, so frame i\n      of the camera == frame i of the imaging unless a trigger was dropped); dropped\n      frames are linearly interpolated, as suggested by the dataset README.\n\nUsage:\n    python -u convert_data.py <outpicklefile> [--full | --sample] [--show-processing]\n\"\"\"\n\nimport argparse\nimport os\nimport pickle\nimport sys\nimport time\nfrom concurrent.futures import ProcessPoolExecutor\n\nimport numpy as np\nfrom scipy.ndimage import gaussian_filter, maximum_filter1d, minimum_filter1d\n\nDATA_DIR = '/app/data'\n\n# --- fixed processing parameters -------------------------------------------------\nBIN_FRAMES = 10            # paper: \"averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps\"\nTRIAL_SEC = 60.0           # task specification: 60 s trials\nN_QUANTILES = 5            # task specification: 5 equal-percentile bins\nSIG_BASELINE = 10.0        # suite2p default (frames)\nWIN_BASELINE = 60.0        # suite2p default (s)\n\n# subject -> (paper mouse label, postnatal day of the first session)\n# Postnatal days are read from Fig. 5B of the paper (mice are named in alphabetically\n# increasing order: jm031 = mouse A ... jm046 = mouse F).\nSUBJECT_INFO = {\n    'jm031': ('A', 7),\n    'jm032': ('B', 7),\n    'jm038': ('C', 8),\n    'jm039': ('D', 8),\n    'jm040': ('E', 9),\n    'jm046': ('F', 8),\n}\nSUBJECTS = list(SUBJECT_INFO.keys())\n\nBRAIN_REGION = 'S1 barrel cortex'\nOUTPUT_VALUES = ['0-20%', '20-40%', '40-60%', '60-80%', '80-100%']\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# loading\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ndef list_sessions(subject):\n    \"\"\"Session directories of one subject, chronologically sorted.\"\"\"\n    d = os.path.join(DATA_DIR, subject)\n    return sorted(f.path for f in os.scandir(d) if f.is_dir())\n\n\ndef load_session_raw(session_dir):\n    \"\"\"Load the raw arrays of one session.\n\n    Returns F, Fneu (n_neurons, n_frames), fs, n_frames.\n    \"\"\"\n    p = os.path.join(session_dir, 'suite2p', 'plane0')\n    F = np.load(os.path.join(p, 'F.npy'))\n    Fneu = np.load(os.path.join(p, 'Fneu.npy'))\n    iscell = np.load(os.path.join(p, 'iscell.npy'))\n    ops = np.load(os.path.join(p, 'ops.npy'), allow_pickle=True).item()\n    # The released data contains only Track2p-tracked cells that already passed the\n    # suite2p classifier; assert rather than filter so that any exception is visible.\n    assert np.all(iscell[:, 0] == 1), f'{session_dir}: unexpected non-cell ROIs'\n    assert F.shape == Fneu.shape\n    assert F.shape[1] == ops['nframes'], f'{session_dir}: F length != ops[\"nframes\"]'\n    return F, Fneu, float(ops['fs']), int(ops['nframes']), float(ops['neucoeff'])\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# neural processing\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ndef f_processing(F, Fneu, fs, neucoeff, baseline='maximin',\n                 sig_baseline=SIG_BASELINE, win_baseline=WIN_BASELINE):\n    \"\"\"dF/F as in track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing (suite2p 'maximin').\n\n    Returns (dff, F0) where dff = (F - neucoeff*Fneu) - F0 and F0 is the maximin\n    baseline.  Kept identical to the reference implementation apart from returning\n    the baseline as well (used for the diagnostic plots).\n    \"\"\"\n    Fc = F - neucoeff * Fneu\n    win = int(win_baseline * fs)\n    if baseline == 'maximin':\n        Flow = gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline])\n        Flow = minimum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n        Flow = maximum_filter1d(Flow, win)\n    elif baseline == 'constant':\n        Flow = np.amin(gaussian_filter(Fc, [0., sig_baseline]))\n    else:\n        Flow = 0.\n    return Fc - Flow, Flow\n\n\ndef bin_average(x, k=None):\n    \"\"\"Average non-overlapping bins of k consecutive samples along the last axis.\n\n    Trailing samples that do not fill a bin are dropped.\n    \"\"\"\n    k = BIN_FRAMES if k is None else k\n    n = (x.shape[-1] // k) * k\n    return x[..., :n].reshape(*x.shape[:-1], n // k, k).mean(axis=-1)\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# behaviour processing\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ndef load_motion_energy(session_dir, n_frames):\n    \"\"\"Motion energy on the imaging frame grid.\n\n    The camera is triggered by the 2-photon acquisition, so camera frame i\n    corresponds to imaging frame i as long as no trigger was dropped.  Dropped\n    triggers show up as inter-frame intervals that are an integer multiple of the\n    nominal one; the cumulative sum of the rounded intervals therefore gives the\n    imaging-frame index of every camera frame.  Missing values (dropped frames, and\n    the first frame, for which no frame difference exists) are linearly interpolated,\n    as suggested by the dataset README.\n\n    Returns (motion_energy (n_frames,), invalid mask (n_frames,) bool).\n    \"\"\"\n    md = os.path.join(session_dir, 'move_deve')\n    me = np.load(os.path.join(md, 'motion_energy_glob.npy')).astype(np.float64)\n    itv = np.load(os.path.join(md, 'interframe_int.npy'))\n    assert len(itv) == len(me) - 1\n\n    nominal = np.median(itv)\n    steps = np.round(itv / nominal).astype(np.int64)\n    idx = np.concatenate([[0], np.cumsum(steps)])\n\n    out = np.full(n_frames, np.nan)\n    keep = idx < n_frames                    # a few sessions record past the last 2p frame\n    out[idx[keep]] = me[keep]\n    out[0] = np.nan                          # me[0] is a placeholder (no previous frame)\n\n    missing = np.isnan(out)\n    if missing.all():\n        raise ValueError(f'{session_dir}: no motion energy could be aligned')\n    out[missing] = np.interp(np.flatnonzero(missing), np.flatnonzero(~missing),\n                             out[~missing])\n    return out, missing\n\n\ndef discretize_quantiles(x, n_bins=N_QUANTILES):\n    \"\"\"Assign each value of x to one of n_bins equal-percentile bins.\n\n    Edges are the n_bins-1 interior quantiles of x itself, so the bins hold equal\n    numbers of samples (up to ties).  Returns (labels int64, edges).\n    \"\"\"\n    qs = np.linspace(0, 100, n_bins + 1)[1:-1]\n    edges = np.percentile(x, qs)\n    labels = np.searchsorted(edges, x, side='right').astype(np.int64)\n    return labels, edges\n\n\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\n# per-session conversion\n# ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------\ndef convert_session(session_dir, neural_scaling='center', verbose=True, neucoeff=None):\n    \"\"\"Convert one session to trial lists.\n\n    neural_scaling: how the dF/F is conditioned for the decoder.  For a linear\n        readout with a bias term (which is what the provided decoder fits on top of\n        the per-session projection) subtracting a per-neuron constant and dividing\n        the whole session by one scalar are information-preserving; they only change\n        the conditioning of the optimisation and of the SVD initialisation.\n        'none'   : dF/F as returned by f_processing (arbitrary fluorescence units)\n        'global' : dF/F divided by one scalar per session (its pooled s.d.)\n        'center' : (default) additionally subtract each neuron's session mean, so\n                   that the projection is initialised with the SVD of the\n                   *fluctuations* rather than of the (large, positive) mean offset\n        'zscore' : each neuron's dF/F z-scored within the session; this also\n                   re-weights neurons by 1/s.d., which is not information preserving\n    \"\"\"\n    t0 = time.time()\n    subject = os.path.basename(os.path.dirname(session_dir))\n    sess_name = os.path.basename(session_dir)\n\n    F, Fneu, fs, n_frames, ops_neucoeff = load_session_raw(session_dir)\n    if neucoeff is None:\n        neucoeff = ops_neucoeff\n    t_load = time.time() - t0\n\n    # --- neural: dF/F then 10-frame averaging ---\n    t1 = time.time()\n    dff, F0 = f_processing(F, Fneu, fs, neucoeff)\n    dff_binned = bin_average(dff).astype(np.float32)\n    t_dff = time.time() - t1\n\n    # --- behaviour: motion energy on the imaging grid, then 10-frame averaging ---\n    me, me_missing = load_motion_energy(session_dir, n_frames)\n    me_binned = bin_average(me)\n    # fraction of interpolated (dropped) camera frames contributing to each bin\n    missing_binned = bin_average(me_missing.astype(np.float64))\n\n    # --- cut into trials ---\n    bins_per_trial = int(round(TRIAL_SEC * fs / BIN_FRAMES))\n    n_trials = dff_binned.shape[1] // bins_per_trial\n    n_used = n_trials * bins_per_trial\n    dff_binned = dff_binned[:, :n_used]\n    me_binned = me_binned[:n_used]\n    missing_binned = missing_binned[:n_used]\n\n    # --- neural scaling (see CONVERSION_NOTES.md Step 5) ---\n    if neural_scaling == 'global':\n        scale = float(dff_binned.std())\n        neural_all = dff_binned / scale\n    elif neural_scaling == 'center':\n        scale = float(dff_binned.std())\n        neural_all = (dff_binned - dff_binned.mean(axis=1, keepdims=True)) / scale\n    elif neural_scaling == 'zscore':\n        mu = dff_binned.mean(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n        sd = dff_binned.std(axis=1, keepdims=True)\n        neural_all = (dff_binned - mu) / np.maximum(sd, 1e-6)\n        scale = None\n    elif neural_scaling == 'none':\n        neural_all = dff_binned\n        scale = 1.0\n    else:\n        raise ValueError(neural_scaling)\n    neural_all = neural_all.astype(np.float32)\n\n    # --- output: quintiles of motion energy, thresholds per session ---\n    labels_all, edges = discretize_quantiles(me_binned)\n\n    # --- input: time from session start (s), at the centre of each bin ---\n    bin_dt = BIN_FRAMES / fs\n    time_all = (np.arange(n_used) + 0.5) * bin_dt\n\n    neural, inputs, outputs = [], [], []\n    for tr in range(n_trials):\n        sl = slice(tr * bins_per_trial, (tr + 1) * bins_per_trial)\n        neural.append(np.ascontiguousarray(neural_all[:, sl]))\n        inputs.append(time_all[sl][None, :].astype(np.float32))\n        outputs.append(labels_all[sl][None, :])\n\n    label, p0 = SUBJECT_INFO[subject]\n    day_idx = list_sessions(subject).index(session_dir)\n    info = {\n        'subject': subject,\n        'mouse_label': label,\n        'session': sess_name,\n        'date': sess_name[:10],\n        'day_index': day_idx,\n        'postnatal_day': p0 + day_idx,\n        'n_neurons': int(neural_all.shape[0]),\n        'n_frames': int(n_frames),\n        'fs': fs,\n        'neucoeff': neucoeff,\n        'n_trials': int(n_trials),\n        'session_duration_s': n_frames / fs,\n        'frac_frames_interpolated': float(me_missing.mean()),\n        'motion_energy_quintile_edges': edges.tolist(),\n        'motion_energy_range': [float(me_binned.min()), float(me_binned.max())],\n        'dff_scale_divisor': scale,\n        'frac_bins_with_interpolated_frames': float((missing_binned > 0).mean()),\n    }\n    if verbose:\n        print(f'  {subject}/{sess_name}: {info[\"n_neurons\"]} neurons, {n_trials} trials, '\n              f'P{info[\"postnatal_day\"]}, dropped camera frames '\n              f'{100 * info[\"frac_frames_interpolated\"]:.3f}% '\n              f'[load {t_load:.1f}s, dff {t_dff:.1f}s, total {time.time() - t0:.1f}s]',\n              flush=True)\n\n    diag = None\n    if os.environ.get('T2P_KEEP_DIAG'):\n        diag = dict(F=F, F0=F0, dff=dff, me=me, me_missing=me_missing,\n                    dff_binned=dff_binned, me_binned=me_binned,\n                    labels_all=labels_all, edges=edges, time_all=time_all,\n                    bins_per_trial=bins_per_trial, fs=fs)\n    return dict(neural=neural, input=inputs, output=outputs, info=info,\n                brain_region_idx=np.zeros(neural_all.shape[0], dtype=np.int64)), diag\n\n\ndef _worker(args):\n    session_dir, {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01VkVEWfy7ZNwCVDz4zWj9p8","type":"tool_result","content":"We next applied Track2p to a longitudinal dataset consisting of daily recordings of the same 720×720 μm FOV throughout the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development (postnatal days 8 (P8) to P14, n=7 imaging sessions from one mouse; for more details see Methods.) In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\n\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \n\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\n\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\n\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\n\nVideography\n\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\nPreprocessing videography\n\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\n\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\nCalcium event rates\n\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\n\nDecoding\n\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\n\nL(\\beta) = \\|y - X\\beta\\|_2^2 + \\lambda\\|\\beta\\|_2^2\n\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\n===README===\n# Data for Majnik et al. 2025:\n\nMajnik Jure, Mantez Manon, Zangila Sofia, Bugeon Stéphane, Guignard Leo, Platel Jean-Claude, Cossart Rosa (2025)\nLongitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p eLife 14:RP107540\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.1\n\n\n## Programatically loading data using 'load_data.ipynb':\n\n- Make sure to unzip the file first, this should result in 6 folders, one for each subjects\n- We provide a simple jupyter notebook for loading the data\n- To load data smoothly make sure that the notebook is in the same directory as the 6 subject folders\n- When using the dataset we suggest the users read the notes below and try in parallel to load the data using the notebook\n\n\n## Notes on data organisation:\n\nsubject folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a'):\n- Each subject folder contains a number of session folders, each corresponding to one recording day.\n- The name of the folder corresponds to the the recording date in the YYYY-MM-DD format (the '_a' in the end of the folder name can be ignored)\n- All recordings are done daily, starting between postnatal days P7 and P9 depending on the mouse (for more information see Fig5 B in the paper)\n\nsuite2p sub-sub-folder (e. g. 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p'):\n- Contains the neural data for the successfully tracked neurons for the session day in Suite2p format\n- This neural data is obtained by running Track2p using the 'save the outputs in suite2p format'\n- For more information on the Suite2p format see: https://suite2p.readthedocs.io/en/latest/outputs.html\n- For more information on the Suite2p format output of Track2p see: https://track2p.github.io/outputs_suite2p.html \n- Briefly the data only includes traces for the cells present across all days, meaning that the activity matrix (for example F.npy or spks.npy, are of shape n_neurons x n_tstamps) will have the same number of rows (neurons) each day and the rows will be matched (e. g. first row of matrix 'jm031/2023-10-18_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy' corresponds to the same neuron as the first row of the matrix on the next recording day 'jm031/2023-10-19_a/suite2p/plane0/F.npy')\n- This data can also be visualised using the Suite2p GUI, for example by opening two recordings of the same mouse in two windows - in that case (thanks to the reindexing) the color code will be the same across the two days for the same neuron.\n\nmove_deve sub-sub-folder (e. g. In addition to the calcium indicator (GCaMP8m), we virally expressed a sparse anatomical marker (tdTomato) targeting GABAergic neurons using conditional expression in GAD67-Cre mice. This dual-labelling strategy facilitated reliable FOV identification across sessions and provided an anatomical reference for Track2p’s initial cross-session registration and tracking (Fig. 3, for comparison with tracking based on GCaMP8m registration see the ‘Benchmarking on manually tracked cells’ section). Before running Track2p, each recording was preprocessed using Suite2p for active cell detection (segmentation) and calcium fluorescence trace extraction (see Methods). The outputs of Suite2p (ROIs, traces and FOV images) were then used as inputs to the Track2p algorithm.\n\nWe next analyzed the development of functional properties of the tracked population of neurons across the second postnatal week of mouse barrel cortex development. For this, we used a full dataset of 6 mice imaged daily for a minimum of 6 consecutive days within the second postnatal week (P7 to P14, see Fig. 5B for summary, we used this dataset for all subsequent analyses). On average 526 (± 190 std) neurons per mouse were successfully tracked across all days using Track2p, corresponding to 33 % (± 11 % std) of the neurons detected on the first day for each mouse. \n\nWe assessed behavioural state indirectly using the videos capturing spontaneous mouse movement and quantifying them using a ‘motion energy’ metric.\n\nChronic 2-photon calcium imaging\n\nLongitudinal two-photon calcium imaging was performed for each mouse for at least 6 consecutive days (see Fig. 5 for details). Imaging was performed using a Bruker (Ultima 2P) microscope with a Coherent Mai-Tai excitation laser (950 nm excitation light) and a 16x Nikon objective (NA 0.8). Before detection, emitted light was split into two optical paths each associated with a filter (red and green, 580-620 and 500-550 nm respectively), allowing us to simultaneously record the GCaMP8m and the tdTomato signal. The acquisition was performed using the Prairie view software. All recordings were performed in layer 2/3 (depth between 100-200 µm from the pial surface) with a 720 × 720 µm field of view and 512 × 512 pixel resolution. Imaging rate was 30 Hz (resonant scanner) and each session lasted 20 minutes. All experiments were performed in the dark, under sensory-minimised conditions, with mice being free to spontaneously run on a non-motorised treadmill (Luigs and Neumann). To facilitate alignment and cell tracking, we kept the alignment of the head mount with respect to the microscope fixed across all sessions for the same mouse. To record from the same region across days we manually aligned the imaging plane in x, y and z to best match the reference images of the red channel (tdTomato) from all previous recording days. During the course of each recording, pups were kept warm by a heating element mounted to the imaging setup. After each imaging session, the pups were returned to their home cage with the dam and their littermates.\n\nVideography\n\nAll videography was performed using a Basler camera (Basler ACE2 1920), with an infrared LED light source (ThorLabs 850 nm) pointed at the mouse. Videos were recorded at 30 Hz, with the microscope acquisition acting as a trigger for camera frame acquisition, also allowing for simple synchronisation across the two modalities.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data was preprocessed using the Suite2p pipeline, sequentially performing motion correction, ROI detection, signal extraction and spike deconvolution 23 for each recording separately. Suite2p additionally provides a cell classification feature, providing a probability of a classifier categorising an ROI as being a true cell. We considered all ROIs above the default threshold of 0.5 as true cells. We used baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters) for all subsequent analyses. To facilitate the use of Track2p with other preprocessing pipelines, we implemented a data loader that takes as input three simple NumPy arrays corresponding to ROIs, field of view and traces (for more information see: https://track2p.github.io/run_inputs_and_parameters).\nPreprocessing videography\n\nWe used the global movements of the mouse as a proxy of its arousal state. Similarly as in 62 we quantified these by looking at the pixel-wise difference of consecutive frames in the videography recording. Namely, we first took each two consecutive frames, computed their pixelwise difference. We then squared all individual pixel-wise values and summed across pixels. This yielded a scalar value quantifying the motion of the mouse at each time point, which was used for all subsequent analyses.\n\nFunctional properties of tracked neurons\nCalcium event rates\n\nTo quantify the calcium event rate statistics we used SciPy’s peak detection algorithm (Python). For this we first denoised the traces slightly by averaging using a bin size of 10 frames and then proceeded to detect any peaks with a height and prominence of at least one standard deviation. We then calculated the rate as the number of peaks per minute within the recording. When quantifying the stability of the rates across days we computed the Pearson correlation coefficient across all neurons for each possible combination of sessions recorded from the same mouse.\n\nDecoding\n\nAll decoding was done using linear regression with ridge regularisation (ridge regression) to avoid overfitting given the large number of neurons. Ridge regression optimises the weights (β) that minimise the following loss function:\n\nL(\\beta) = \\|y - X\\beta\\|_2^2 + \\lambda\\|\\beta\\|_2^2\n\nWhere in our case y is behavioural data, X is neural data and λ is a regularisation parameter. To choose the optimal λ and to reliably estimate the model performance on same-day decoding we used nested cross-validation (see for example 65,66), ensuring data efficiency and a splitting between training, validation and test data. Briefly, a grid search for the optimal λ is performed by using the train set to fit models with different λ values and choosing the value corresponding to the model with the best performance on the held out validation set (model selection). This model is then evaluated on the test data that was used neither in model fitting nor in the hyperparameter optimization (model evaluation). To ensure data efficiency, nested cross-validation repeats this procedure for all possible splits of the data, with an outer cross-validation loop used for model evaluation and an inner loop for model selection. We used 5 fold splits for both the inner and outer loops, splits were done on consecutive 2 minute blocks of the recording. For cross-day decoding we fit a new model with the optimal lambda for that given day and evaluated it on all other days. For all decoding analysis we slightly denoised the dF/F as well as the behaviour traces by averaging in bins of 10 consecutive timestamps. All models were implemented and fitted using PyTorch (Python).\n===README===\n# Data for Majnik et al. 2025:\n\nMajnik Jure, Mantez Manon, Zangila Sofia, Bugeon Stéphane, Guignard Leo, Platel Jean-Claude, Cossart Rosa (2025)\nLongitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p eLife 14:RP107540\nhttps://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.1\n\n\n## Programatically loading data using 'load_data.ipynb':\n\n- Make sure to unzip the file first, this should result in 6 folders, one for each subjects\n- We provide a simple jupyter notebook for loading the data\n- To load data smoothly make sure that the notebook is in the same directory as the 6 subject folders\n- When using the dataset we suggest the users read the notes below and try in parallel to load the data using the notebook\n\n\n## Notes on data organisation:\n\nsubject folder (e. g. fluorescence with suite2p defaults — neuropil coefficient, maximin baseline with `sig_baseline=10` frames Gaussian smoothing and a 60 s (1800-frame) min/max filter window.\n- **Motion energy**: pixel-wise difference of consecutive video frames, squared, summed over pixels ⇒ one scalar per frame (already provided in `motion_energy_glob.npy`).\n- **Temporal alignment**: the 2-photon acquisition triggers the camera, so camera frame *i* ≙ imaging frame *i*, up to occasionally dropped camera triggers (README: \"the indices of missing frames can be obtained by looking at 'tstamps.npy' or 'interframe_int.npy' and treated as missing values for motion energy or they can be interpolated over\").\n- **Temporal binning for decoding**: 10-frame (333.33 ms) box average of both dF/F and behaviour.\n- **Decoding cross-validation** in the paper: splits on consecutive **2-minute blocks** of the recording (nested 5-fold). Here the task specifies 60 s trials, and `train_validate_decoder` splits trials 80/20 — same idea (contiguous blocks), finer.\n\n### Curation Steps\n**Neuron curation rules**: suite2p classifier `iscell` > 0.5 (done before release: all released ROIs have `iscell[:,0]==1`), and kept only if tracked by Track2p on *all* days of that mouse (also done before release). ⇒ **no additional neuron filtering applied here.**\n\n**Trial curation rules**: the paper uses the whole continuous recording; there are no behavioural trials. The only data-quality issue is dropped camera frames (≤0.41 % of frames per session), which are interpolated over as suggested by the data README. No session or trial is dropped: every session has both neural and behavioural data of the full length.\n\n### Decoders Trained\n| Decoded variable | Accuracy |\n|---|---|\n| Mouse motion (motion energy), same-day ridge regression | R² per session, ≈0.15 median early (≤P11) and ≈0.55 median late (>P11), range −0.1…0.8 (Fig. 7C,H) |\n| Mouse motion, cross-day | early→early ≈0.1, late→late ≈0.45, early↔late ≈0.1 (Fig. 7H) |\n(The paper reports no categorical decoding accuracy — it regresses continuous motion energy. The R² values set the expectation that a 5-class decoder should be clearly above the 0.2 chance level, with strong session-to-session variability by age.)\n\n---\n## Step 4: Check for Consistency\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nEverything in the reference code, the data and the paper agrees except for the points below.\n\n### Discrepancies Found\n| Topic | Code says | Data shows | Paper says | Resolution |\n|-------|-----------|------------|------------|------------|\n| Number of tracked cells per mouse | n/a (tracking already applied) | A 221, B 370, C 685, D 746, E 541, F 435 (mean 499.7, std 197.7) | Fig. 5B: A 285, B 376, C 799, D 728, E 541, F 411 (mean 523, text \"526 ± 190\") | The released dataset (v1, 2025-09) differs by −22 %…+2.5 % per mouse from the figure of the paper (v2); mouse E matches exactly. The released suite2p folders are the ground truth we must convert, so **the data is used as-is**; the per-mouse ordering, the number of mice, the number of days and the std (197.7 vs 190) all agree. Most likely the released version was re-run / re-curated after the figure was made. |\n| Session duration | n/a | jm031, jm032: 36 000 frames = 20 min; the other 4 mice: 54 000 frames = 30 min | \"each session lasted 20 minutes\" | The data wins: sessions are 20 or 30 min. Trial count per session follows the actual length (20 or 30 trials of 60 s). Fig. 5A also plots only the first 20 min of the (30 min) example-mouse recording, consistent with 30 min recordings existing. |\n| Neuropil coefficient for dF/F | `F_processing(..., neucoeff=0.0)` is how the track2p GUI calls it | `ops['neucoeff'] = 0.7` in every session (suite2p default actually used to preprocess these recordings) | \"baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F (using the default Suite2p parameters)\" | Used **0.7** (`ops['neucoeff']`), i.e. the suite2p default that the paper refers to. Checked empirically that the choice is immaterial: ridge R² of motion energy (paper Fig. 7C) is early 0.111 / late 0.453 with 0.7 vs early 0.121 / late 0.466 with 0.0; Ca²⁺ event rates for the example mouse match Fig. 5D slightly better with 0.7. |\n| Is dF/F divided by F0? | `F_processing` returns `Fc − F0` (no division) | — | \"baseline corrected fluorescence traces as our dF/F\" | Followed the reference code: **no division by F0**. Dividing was tested and is actively harmful here: after neuropil subtraction F0 can be ≈0 for dim cells, which explodes those traces (e.g. jm040 P14 PC1–motion correlation collapses from 0.69 to 0.02). |\n| Number of days per mouse | — | 7,7,7,7,**6**,7 | \"minimum of 6 consecutive days\"; Fig. 5B shows mouse E starting at P9 (6 days) | Consistent — mouse E = jm040 has 6 sessions. |\n| Dropped camera frames | — | 0–148 frames per session missing from `motion_energy_glob.npy` | data README: \"can be treated as missing values … or they can be interpolated over\" | Reconstructed the imaging-frame index of every camera frame from `interframe_int.npy` and linearly interpolated the (always single-frame) gaps. |\n\nAdditional consistency checks run at this stage (details in Step 10):\n- Ca²⁺ event rate per session reproduces Fig. 5D (example mouse D: ours 3.4→6.0 /min from P8→P14, paper 3.0→6.9).\n- PC1–motion correlation reproduces Fig. 7D (near 0 up to P11, steep rise afterwards, max 0.64–0.78).\n- Ridge-regression R² of motion energy reproduces Fig. 7C/H (early median 0.111, late median 0.453; paper ≈0.15 / ≈0.55).\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops['neucoeff','fs']` | `neural` | `F_processing` (maximin dF/F) → average of 10 consecutive frames → per-neuron mean subtraction → divide by one scalar per session → cut into 60 s (180-bin) trials | `track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing`; `load_data.ipynb:load_traces` | (n_neurons, 180) float32 per trial |\n| frame index | `input[0]` \"time from session start (s)\" | `(bin_index + 0.5) * 10 / 30` s (bin centre) | — | (1, 180) float32, ramps 0.17 → 1199.83 or 1799.83 s |\n| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` + `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` \"motion energy quintile\" | align to imaging frames, interpolate dropped frames, average 10 frames, discretise at the session's 20/40/60/80th percentiles | data README; Methods \"Preprocessing videography\" | (1, 180) int64, values 0–4 |\n| folder name (`jm031`…) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | — | data README | 6 mice, ordered as in the paper (A…F) |\n| — | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | all neurons are L2/3 barrel cortex | Methods | single region, index 0 |\n| `ops`, folder names, Fig. 5B | `metadata['session_info']` | date, postnatal day, n neurons, dropped-frame fraction, quintile edges, … | Fig. 5B for P-day of first session | |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Neural signal = dF/F, not `spks`**: the paper states that all analyses (including decoding) use the baseline-corrected fluorescence; `spks.npy` (deconvolved) is never used for the decoding figures.\n2. **dF/F exactly as `F_processing`** with suite2p defaults (`neucoeff = ops['neucoeff'] = 0.7`, `sig_baseline = 10`, `win_baseline = 60 s`, maximin), computed on the **whole session** before trial-cutting so that the baseline is not distorted at trial edges.\n3. **Time bin = 10 frames = 333.33 ms**: exactly the denoising the paper applies \"for all decoding analysis\" to both dF/F and behaviour. (Tested 1 s bins: validation accuracy 0.325 vs 0.322 — no material gain, so the paper-matching bin was kept.)\n4. **Trial = 60 s = 180 bins**, as specified by the task; 20 trials for the 20 min sessions, 30 for the 30 min ones. This mirrors the paper's own cross-validation, which splits the recording into contiguous 2 min blocks.\n5. **Per-neuron mean subtraction + one scalar per session**: for a linear readout *with a bias* (which is what the provided decoder fits on top of the per-session projection) both operations are information-preserving; they only improve the conditioning of the optimisation and make the SVD initialisation describe the *fluctuations* rather than the large positive mean offset. Measured effect on validation balanced accuracy (full dataset): raw dF/F 0.301, +per-session scalar only 0.291–0.309 (4 runs), +centring 0.302–0.323 (5 runs), per-neuron z-score 0.317 (but z-scoring re-weights neurons by 1/s.d., which is *not* information preserving, and overfits more: train 0.64). Centring was chosen.\n6. **No neuron curation**: the released suite2p folders already contain only ROIs with `iscell == 1` that Track2p matched on *every* day of that mouse (verified: `iscell[:,0]` is 1 for all 20 445 ROIs, and the row count is identical across the days of a mouse). No further quality criterion is available or appropriate.\n7. **No trial/session curation**: there is no task, so no trial can be \"bad\"; every session has complete neural and behavioural coverage. The only defect is dropped camera frames (≤0.41 % of a session, always single frames), interpolated rather than dropped so that all trials keep the same length.\n8. **Motion energy alignment**: camera frame *i* ≙ imaging frame *i* (the microscope triggers the camera). Dropped triggers are located from `interframe_int.npy` (every gap is exactly 2× the nominal interval) and the values after a gap are shifted accordingly. `motion_energy_glob[0]` is a placeholder (no preceding frame) and is treated as missing.\n9. **Quintiles per session** (as the task requires): thresholds are the 20/40/60/80th percentiles of that session's binned motion energy, so each class holds exactly 20 % of the bins of every session.\n10. **Output is time-varying** (one label per 333 ms bin) rather than one label per trial, as the task instructs (\"If at all possible, make it time-varying\").\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [x] 41 sessions / 6 mice / 1090 trials / 20 445 neurons, n_neurons constant within a mouse and equal to `F.npy`'s row count\n- [x] Spot-check `neural[s][trial][neuron, bin]` against an independently recomputed dF/F\n- [x] Spot-check the time ramp of `input`\n- [x] Rebuild the quintile labels with a rank-based method (no `np.percentile`) and compare\n- [x] Each class holds 20 % of the bins in every session\n- [x] Neural–behaviour cross-correlation peaks at ≈0 lag; a deliberate 33 s shift destroys it\n- [x] Drop-corrected alignment beats naive alignment in the two sessions with >100 dropped frames\n- [x] Reproduce paper Fig. 5D (event rates), Fig. 7D (PC1–motion correlation), Fig. 7C/H (ridge R²)\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n`/app/convert_data.py` — `python -u convert_data.py <out.pkl> [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]`.\nExtra options used for the documented experiments: `--neural-scaling {none,global,center,zscore}` (default `center`), `--neucoeff` (default `ops['neucoeff']`), `--bin-frames` (default 10), `--workers`.\n\nStructure: `load_session_raw` → `f_processing` (copied from the reference GUI code) → `bin_average` → `load_motion_energy` (alignment + interpolation) → `discretize_quantiles` → trial cutting → assembly + assertions.\n\nCode inefficiencies identified: `ops.npy` is 85 MB per session (mean images) but only `fs`/`nframes`/`neucoeff` are needed; the maximin baseline over (n_neurons × 54 000) is the dominant cost (~0.8 s).\n\nCode speedups added: sessions are converted in parallel with a `ProcessPoolExecutor` (8 workers); all filtering/averaging is vectorised (`reshape(...).mean(-1)` instead of loops); float32 throughout. Full dataset converts in **6 s**.\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nSample = one early 20 min session (jm031 / 2023-10-18, mouse A, P7) and one late 30 min session (jm046 / 2024-09-08, mouse F, P13), chosen to cover both session lengths and both ends of the developmental range.\n\n### Sample Statistics\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 656 (221 + 435) |\n| Neurons / session | 221, 435 |\n| Subjects | 2 of 6 |\n| Sessions / subject | 1 |","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"1b18555b-8c1d-4069-b551-dc869513c678","uuid":"7cb7fce1-c672-453c-9c8a-f9b02f49c22d","timestamp":"2026-09-19T20:26:11.335Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"391 /app/CONVERSION_NOTES.md\n# Dataset Conversion Notes\n\n## Overview\n- **Dataset**: Majnik et al. 2025 (eLife 107540) — \"Longitudinal tracking of neuronal activity from the same cells in the developing brain using Track2p\". paper ≈0.15 / ≈0.55).\n\n---\n\n## Step 5: Mapping Planning\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Variable Mapping\n| Source Variable | Target Field | Transform | Reference Code Function(s) | Notes |\n|-----------------|--------------|-----------|----------------------------|-------|\n| `suite2p/plane0/F.npy`, `Fneu.npy`, `ops['neucoeff','fs']` | `neural` | `F_processing` (maximin dF/F) → average of 10 consecutive frames → per-neuron mean subtraction → divide by one scalar per session → cut into 60 s (180-bin) trials | `track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing`; `load_data.ipynb:load_traces` | (n_neurons, 180) float32 per trial |\n| frame index | `input[0]` \"time from session start (s)\" | `(bin_index + 0.5) * 10 / 30` s (bin centre) | — | (1, 180) float32, ramps 0.17 → 1199.83 or 1799.83 s |\n| `move_deve/motion_energy_glob.npy` + `interframe_int.npy` | `output[0]` \"motion energy quintile\" | align to imaging frames, interpolate dropped frames, average 10 frames, discretise at the session's 20/40/60/80th percentiles | data README; Methods \"Preprocessing videography\" | (1, 180) int64, values 0–4 |\n| folder name (`jm031`…) | `subjects`, `subject_idx` | — | data README | 6 mice, ordered as in the paper (A…F) |\n| — | `brain_regions`, `brain_region_idx` | all neurons are L2/3 barrel cortex | Methods | single region, index 0 |\n| `ops`, folder names, Fig. 5B | `metadata['session_info']` | date, postnatal day, n neurons, dropped-frame fraction, quintile edges, … | Fig. 5B for P-day of first session | |\n\n### Key Decisions\n1. **Neural signal = dF/F, not `spks`**: the paper states that all analyses (including decoding) use the baseline-corrected fluorescence; `spks.npy` (deconvolved) is never used for the decoding figures.\n2. **dF/F exactly as `F_processing`** with suite2p defaults (`neucoeff = ops['neucoeff'] = 0.7`, `sig_baseline = 10`, `win_baseline = 60 s`, maximin), computed on the **whole session** before trial-cutting so that the baseline is not distorted at trial edges.\n3. **Time bin = 10 frames = 333.33 ms**: exactly the denoising the paper applies \"for all decoding analysis\" to both dF/F and behaviour. (Tested 1 s bins: validation accuracy 0.325 vs 0.322 — no material gain, so the paper-matching bin was kept.)\n4. **Trial = 60 s = 180 bins**, as specified by the task; 20 trials for the 20 min sessions, 30 for the 30 min ones. This mirrors the paper's own cross-validation, which splits the recording into contiguous 2 min blocks.\n5. **Per-neuron mean subtraction + one scalar per session**: for a linear readout *with a bias* (which is what the provided decoder fits on top of the per-session projection) both operations are information-preserving; they only improve the conditioning of the optimisation and make the SVD initialisation describe the *fluctuations* rather than the large positive mean offset. Measured effect on validation balanced accuracy (full dataset): raw dF/F 0.301, +per-session scalar only 0.291–0.309 (4 runs), +centring 0.302–0.323 (5 runs), per-neuron z-score 0.317 (but z-scoring re-weights neurons by 1/s.d., which is *not* information preserving, and overfits more: train 0.64). Centring was chosen.\n6. **No neuron curation**: the released suite2p folders already contain only ROIs with `iscell == 1` that Track2p matched on *every* day of that mouse (verified: `iscell[:,0]` is 1 for all 20 445 ROIs, and the row count is identical across the days of a mouse). No further quality criterion is available or appropriate.\n7. **No trial/session curation**: there is no task, so no trial can be \"bad\"; every session has complete neural and behavioural coverage. The only defect is dropped camera frames (≤0.41 % of a session, always single frames), interpolated rather than dropped so that all trials keep the same length.\n8. **Motion energy alignment**: camera frame *i* ≙ imaging frame *i* (the microscope triggers the camera). Dropped triggers are located from `interframe_int.npy` (every gap is exactly 2× the nominal interval) and the values after a gap are shifted accordingly. `motion_energy_glob[0]` is a placeholder (no preceding frame) and is treated as missing.\n9. **Quintiles per session** (as the task requires): thresholds are the 20/40/60/80th percentiles of that session's binned motion energy, so each class holds exactly 20 % of the bins of every session.\n10. **Output is time-varying** (one label per 333 ms bin) rather than one label per trial, as the task instructs (\"If at all possible, make it time-varying\").\n\n### Planned Sanity Checks\n- [x] 41 sessions / 6 mice / 1090 trials / 20 445 neurons, n_neurons constant within a mouse and equal to `F.npy`'s row count\n- [x] Spot-check `neural[s][trial][neuron, bin]` against an independently recomputed dF/F\n- [x] Spot-check the time ramp of `input`\n- [x] Rebuild the quintile labels with a rank-based method (no `np.percentile`) and compare\n- [x] Each class holds 20 % of the bins in every session\n- [x] Neural–behaviour cross-correlation peaks at ≈0 lag; a deliberate 33 s shift destroys it\n- [x] Drop-corrected alignment beats naive alignment in the two sessions with >100 dropped frames\n- [x] Reproduce paper Fig. 5D (event rates), Fig. 7D (PC1–motion correlation), Fig. 7C/H (ridge R²)\n\n---\n\n## Step 6: Script Development\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n`/app/convert_data.py` — `python -u convert_data.py <out.pkl> [--full|--sample] [--show-processing]`.\nExtra options used for the documented experiments: `--neural-scaling {none,global,center,zscore}` (default `center`), `--neucoeff` (default `ops['neucoeff']`), `--bin-frames` (default 10), `--workers`.\n\nStructure: `load_session_raw` → `f_processing` (copied from the reference GUI code) → `bin_average` → `load_motion_energy` (alignment + interpolation) → `discretize_quantiles` → trial cutting → assembly + assertions.\n\nCode inefficiencies identified: `ops.npy` is 85 MB per session (mean images) but only `fs`/`nframes`/`neucoeff` are needed; the maximin baseline over (n_neurons × 54 000) is the dominant cost (~0.8 s).\n\nCode speedups added: sessions are converted in parallel with a `ProcessPoolExecutor` (8 workers); all filtering/averaging is vectorised (`reshape(...).mean(-1)` instead of loops); float32 throughout. Full dataset converts in **6 s**.\n\n---\n\n## Step 7: Sample Conversion and Validation\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\nSample = one early 20 min session (jm031 / 2023-10-18, mouse A, P7) and one late 30 min session (jm046 / 2024-09-08, mouse F, P13), chosen to cover both session lengths and both ends of the developmental range.\n\n### Sample Statistics\n| Statistic | Value |\n|-----------|-------|\n| Neurons (total) | 656 (221 + 435) |\n| Neurons / session | 221, 435 |\n| Subjects | 2 of 6 |\n| Sessions / subject | 1 |","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

Every statistic that exists in both the paper and the data matches, except the per-mouse cell counts of Fig. 5B (released data differs from the figure; the data is authoritative and was investigated in Step 4). Three independent paper figures (5D, 7C, 7D) were quantitatively reproduced from the converted arrays.\n\n### Check 5 — Edge cases handled\n- `motion_energy_glob[0]` is a placeholder 0 (no preceding video frame) → treated as missing and interpolated, in every session.\n- Three jm046 sessions have a camera that kept running past the last 2-photon frame (reconstructed index > `nframes`): those samples are discarded (`idx < n_frames`).\n- Two sessions drop >100 camera frames; all gaps are exactly one frame (ratio 2.00, never ambiguous: no interval falls between 1.2× and 1.8× the nominal one).\n- Sessions are 36 000 or 54 000 frames = exactly 20 or 30 whole trials, so no partial trial is produced; the code still floors to whole trials and drops the remainder if a future session did not divide evenly.\n- `assert`s on `iscell`, on `F.shape == Fneu.shape`, on `F.shape[1] == ops['nframes']` and on `len(interframe_int) == len(motion_energy) − 1` make any deviation loud rather than silent.\n- Final structure assertions in `main()` re-check shapes, finiteness and label range for all 1090 trials.\n\n### Issues Found and Resolved\n1. *Cross-correlation sanity check initially failed* (peak at +5 bins). Investigated: (i) the check itself used a crude `np.resize` alignment which breaks precisely in the two sessions with >100 dropped frames, and (ii) raw motion energy is so heavy-tailed that the cross-correlation argmax is unstable. Fixed the check (proper alignment, log-transformed motion energy); peaks then fall 1–3 bins after zero, exactly what GCaMP kinetics predict. Added a negative control and a naive-vs-corrected comparison to prove the alignment is what carries the correlation. **No change to the conversion was needed.**\n2. *dF/F divided by F0 explodes for dim cells* (after neuropil subtraction F0 can be ~0). Resolved by following the reference implementation, which does not divide.\n3. *Neuron-count arithmetic error in the Step 2 notes* (21 635) — recomputed: 20 445, matching the converter's own count.\n\n---\n\n## Step 11: Full Decoder Training\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n`python -u train_decoder.py /app/converted_data.pkl --plot-samples` → `/app/train_decoder_full_out.txt` (ran on the L4 GPU, ~40 s).\n\n### Training Progress\n- Loss decreasing: **Yes** (30.8 → 6.1 → 1.57 → 1.41 → 1.34 at epochs 10/50/100/150/200; test loss 2.06)\n\n### Decoder Results (Full)\n| Output | Training Balanced Acc | Validation Balanced Acc | Chance | Notes |\n|--------|-------------|--------|--------|-------|\n| motion energy quintile | 0.615 | 0.322 | 0.200 | 1.61× chance; strongly age dependent (see Step 12) |\n\n---\n\n## Step 12: Critical Review 2\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n### Check 1 — Accuracy vs chance\n| Variable | Validation balanced acc | Chance (1/5) | Ratio |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| motion energy quintile | 0.322 | 0.200 | 1.61× |\n\nAbove chance for the only output, and above the 1.5× threshold. Per-session validation accuracy (`cache/persession_acc_out.txt`) ranges from 0.21 to 0.52 and increases with age exactly as the paper's decoding analysis does (early ≤P11 mean 0.304, late >P11 mean 0.333; the best sessions are jm046 P13/P14 at 0.44/0.52, which are also the sessions with the highest PC1–motion correlation, 0.64/0.59).\n\n### Check 2 — Accuracy comparison to the paper\nThe paper never reports a classification accuracy: it regresses *continuous* motion energy with ridge regression and reports R². The comparable quantity was therefore computed on the converted data with the paper's own protocol (nested 5-fold CV, contiguous 2 min blocks, ridge):\n\n| Quantity | Paper | This conversion |\n|---|---|---|\n| Same-day decoding R², early (≤P11), median | ≈0.15 (Fig. 7C,H) | 0.111 |\n| Same-day decoding R², late (>P11), median | ≈0.55 (Fig. 7C,H) | 0.453 |\n| Best session R² | ≈0.8 (Fig. 7C) | 0.767 (jm046 P14) |\n| Example mouse D, P8 → P14 | 0.26 → 0.69 (Fig. 7B symbols) | 0.061 → 0.577 |\n| Ca²⁺ event rate, mouse D, P8→P14 | 3.0 → 6.9 /min (Fig. 5D) | 3.4 → 6.0 /min |\n| PC1–motion correlation, late epoch | median ≈0.35, max ≈0.7 (Fig. 7D) | median 0.26, max 0.64 |\n\nThe paper's numbers are reproduced within the difference expected from the released dataset containing a slightly different set of tracked cells (Step 4). This is the strongest available evidence that the neural, behavioural and temporal-alignment parts of the conversion are correct: the information the paper extracted is present in the converted arrays.\n\n### Check 3 — Train vs validation gap\nTraining 0.615 vs validation 0.322 (1.9×). Investigated and attributed to the decoder, not to the data:\n- The decoder fits a *separate* 100 × n_neurons projection per session (≈2·10⁶ parameters for 41 sessions) on 157 k training bins, so per-session overfitting is expected.\n- Data leakage is impossible: trials are contiguous, non-overlapping 60 s blocks, and the split is made over whole trials. The only shared quantities are the per-session quintile thresholds and the per-session dF/F scale, both of which are properties of the session, not of individual trials (the paper likewise standardises per session).\n- The gap tracks the amount of per-neuron re-weighting allowed: raw dF/F 0.53/0.31, centred 0.61/0.32, z-scored 0.64/0.32 — validation is flat while training rises, i.e. the extra fit is pure overfitting and does not cost validation accuracy.\n\n### Additional debugging performed\n1. Output values verified against the raw data for specific trials (Step 10, Check 2) — labels reproduce a rank-based quintile assignment exactly.\n2. Temporal alignment verified by cross-correlation, negative control and the naive-alignment comparison (Step 10, Check 2), plus the per-trial plots in `processing_*.png`.\n3. Output variation: every session has exactly 20 % of bins in each of the 5 classes — no degenerate class.\n4. Neural filtering: all released ROIs pass `iscell`, all are tracked on every day; nothing further to filter.\n5. Processing matched to the reference code function by function (Step 10, Check 3).\n\n### Things tried that did *not* improve the decoder (and were therefore not adopted)\n| Variant | Validation balanced acc |\n|---|---|\n| **dF/F, centred, one scalar per session (adopted)** | **0.302 / 0.315 / 0.318 / 0.322 / 0.323** (5 runs) |\n| dF/F, one scalar per session, no centring | 0.291 / 0.294 / 0.297 / 0.309 |\n| raw dF/F, no centring or scaling | 0.301 |\n| per-neuron z-score | 0.317 (train 0.642 — more overfitting) |\n| 1 s bins (30 frames) instead of the paper's 333 ms | 0.325 — within run-to-run noise, and it departs from the paper |\n| neuropil coefficient 0.0 instead of 0.7 | ridge R² early 0.121 / late 0.466 (vs 0.111 / 0.453) — immaterial |\n\n### Issues Found and Resolved\n- *Validation accuracy initially 0.309 with an SVD initialisation dominated by the mean offset*: resolved by subtracting each neuron's session mean (information-preserving for a biased linear readout), which raised validation accuracy to 0.302–0.323 across runs and made the loss curve start an order of magnitude lower.\n- No other issue survived the checks above.\n\n---\n\n## Step 13: Documentation and Cleanup\n**Status**: COMPLETE\n\n- [x] README.md created\n- [x] cache/ folder created (analysis scripts + their saved outputs, documented in `cache/README_CACHE.md`)\n- [x] All files organized","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01RN8gQDk8Rn3iGEJMreMT6a","type":"tool_result","content":"# Decoding mouse movement from developing barrel cortex (Majnik et al. 2025, Track2p)\n\n`converted_data.pkl` holds the longitudinal two-photon dataset of\n[Majnik et al., eLife 14:RP107540](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.1) reformatted for the\nneural-decoder harness in `train_decoder.py`.\n\n## Dataset\n\nHead-fixed mouse pups run spontaneously on a non-motorised treadmill in the dark, under\nsensory-minimised conditions, while layer 2/3 of the barrel cortex is imaged at 30 Hz\n(GCaMP8m, 720 × 720 µm FOV). There is no task and no stimulus. An infrared camera, triggered\nby the microscope, films the mouse at 30 Hz; the squared pixel-wise difference between\nconsecutive frames, summed over pixels (\"motion energy\"), is the behavioural read-out.\n\nEach mouse was imaged daily for 6–7 consecutive days during the second postnatal week\n(P7–P14). Track2p matched the same neurons across all days of a mouse, so within a subject\nevery session contains the *same* neurons in the *same* row order.\n\n| | |\n|---|---|\n| Subjects | 6 (`jm031`…`jm046` = mice A…F of the paper) |\n| Sessions | 41 (7, 7, 7, 7, 6, 7 per mouse), one per day, P7–P14 |\n| Session length | 20 min (mice A, B) or 30 min (mice C–F) |\n| Neurons per mouse | 221, 370, 685, 746, 541, 435 (20 445 session-neurons in total) |\n| Trials | 1090 contiguous 60 s blocks (20 or 30 per session) |\n| Time bin | 333.33 ms (10 imaging frames), 180 bins per trial |\n| Brain region | S1 barrel cortex, layer 2/3 |\n\n## Decoder task\n\n* **input** (`input_names = ['time from session start (s)']`): elapsed time at the centre of\n  each bin, 0.17 … 1199.83 s or 1799.83 s. Shape `(1, 180)` per trial.\n* **output** (`output_names = ['motion energy quintile']`): motion energy discretised into 5\n  equal-percentile bins, thresholds computed **per session**, so each class holds exactly 20 %\n  of a session's bins. Values 0–4 with `output_values[0] = ['0-20%', …, '80-100%']`.\n  Shape `(1, 180)` per trial.\n* **neural**: dF/F of the tracked neurons, shape `(n_neurons, 180)` per trial, `float32`.\n\n## Processing\n\n1. **dF/F** — `(F − 0.7·Fneu)` minus the suite2p *maximin* baseline (Gaussian σ = 10 frames,\n   then a 60 s minimum filter followed by a 60 s maximum filter), i.e. the reference\n   implementation `track2p/gui/data_management.py:F_processing` with the suite2p defaults\n   stored in `ops`. Computed on the whole session, before trials are cut.\n2. **Binning** — dF/F and motion energy are averaged in bins of 10 consecutive frames, the\n   denoising the paper applies \"for all decoding analysis\".\n3. **Conditioning** — each neuron's session mean is subtracted and the session is divided by\n   one scalar (its pooled s.d.). For a linear readout with a bias term both operations are\n   information-preserving; they only improve the conditioning of the decoder's optimisation.\n   The divisor is stored per session in `metadata['session_info'][i]['dff_scale_divisor']`.\n4. **Behaviour alignment** — camera frame *i* corresponds to imaging frame *i*; dropped camera\n   triggers are located from `interframe_int.npy` and the following samples shifted\n   accordingly, then the (always single-frame) gaps are linearly interpolated. Dropped frames\n   are ≤ 0.41 % of a session.\n5. **Trials** — the session is cut into consecutive, non-overlapping 60 s blocks.\n\nNo neurons, trials or sessions are discarded: the released suite2p folders already contain only\nROIs that passed the suite2p classifier *and* were tracked by Track2p on every day.\n\n## Using the data","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"1b18555b-8c1d-4069-b551-dc869513c678","uuid":"95c975f8-8690-499b-af07-4ba99038c6a5","timestamp":"2026-09-19T20:26:59.308Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"# Decoding mouse movement from developing barrel cortex (Majnik et al. 2025, Track2p)\n\n`converted_data.pkl` holds the longitudinal two-photon dataset of\n[Majnik et al., eLife 14:RP107540](https://doi.org/10.7554/eLife.107540.1) reformatted for the\nneural-decoder harness in `train_decoder.py`.\n\n## Dataset\n\nHead-fixed mouse pups run spontaneously on a non-motorised treadmill in the dark, under\nsensory-minimised conditions, while layer 2/3 of the barrel cortex is imaged at 30 Hz\n(GCaMP8m, 720 × 720 µm FOV).
